\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 25.58 0 C 25.58 1.08 24.7 1.96 23.62 1.96 C 22.54 1.96 21.67 1.08 21.67 0 C 21.67 -1.08 22.54 -1.96 23.62 -1.96 C 24.7 -1.96 25.58 -1.08 25.58 0 Z M 23.62 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{17.07182pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 37.39 0 C 37.39 1.08 36.51 1.96 35.43 1.96 C 34.35 1.96 33.48 1.08 33.48 0 C 33.48 -1.08 34.35 -1.96 35.43 -1.96 C 36.51 -1.96 37.39 -1.08 37.39 0 Z M 35.43 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{25.6073pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 25.58 19.69 C 25.58 20.77 24.7 21.64 23.62 21.64 C 22.54 21.64 21.67 20.77 21.67 19.69 C 21.67 18.6 22.54 17.73 23.62 17.73 C 24.7 17.73 25.58 18.6 25.58 19.69 Z M 23.62 19.69}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{17.07182pt}{14.22638pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\quad+\quad\ldots

(96)

However, all of them are equivalent to the diagrams previously studied.
Carefully combining the results gives

\displaystyle F_{\phi^{2}\phi^{2}}(r,w)

\displaystyle=a^{2}_{\phi^{2}}+\xi^{-\Delta_{\phi^{2}}}-{\varepsilon}\,\frac{N+2}{2N}\,\xi^{-1}\log\frac{4r}{\left(1+r\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{N+8}{2N}\left(1+{\varepsilon}\log 2-{\varepsilon}\,\frac{N^{2}+23N+54}{2(N+8)^{2}}\right)\xi^{{\Delta_{\phi}}-{\Delta_{\phi^{2}}}}

\displaystyle\quad+{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{3N+24}{8N}+\frac{N+2}{2N}\,\xi^{-1}\right)H(r,w)+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,.

(97)

As a final comment, observe that for
N>1
both
F_{\phi\phi^{2}}
and
F_{\phi^{2}\phi^{2}}
would be difficult to compute using bootstrap methods, since one needs to first solve a mixing problem.
Therefore, from the bootstrap point of view it is surprising that the two-point functions (
92
), (
95
) and (
97
) have such a similar structure, and it would be interesting to understand if there is a CFT argument for why this should be the case.

4
OPE analysis

In section
3
we determined two-point functions in the presence of a localized magnetic field line defect in the
{\varepsilon}
–expansion.
These correlation functions contain CFT data for several infinite families of operators, as we discuss below.
However, to extract the physically interesting one-point coefficients
a_{\mathcal{O}}
, we need to remove the an extra three-point coefficient
\lambda_{12{\mathcal{O}}}
.
For this reason, we start in section
4.1
with a review of the Wilson-Fisher fixed point without a defect.
We are then ready to present bulk data in section
4.2
and defect data in section
4.3
, paying particular attention to families of non-degenerate operators.

4.1
Review of Wilson-Fisher CFT data

We start reviewing the lowest-twist trajectories for the Wilson-Fisher
O(N)
model, but we refer to
[
55
]
for a more thorough discussion.
The fundamental field
\phi_{a}(x)
, which transforms as a vector under
O(N)
, has the same dimension as a free field at the order we are working

\displaystyle{\Delta_{\phi}}=\frac{d-2}{2}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,.

(98)

We are interested on the operators that appear in the OPE of
\phi_{a}\times\phi_{b}
that transform as singlets or rank-two symmetric-traceless tensors.
The simplest operators are
\phi_{c}^{2}
and
T_{ab}=\phi_{a}\phi_{b}-\frac{\delta_{ab}}{N}\phi_{c}^{2}
, whose dimension and OPE coefficients read

\displaystyle\Delta_{\phi^{2}}

\displaystyle=2\Delta_{\phi}+\gamma_{\phi^{2}}{\varepsilon}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,,\qquad\lambda_{\phi\phi\phi^{2}}^{2}=\frac{2}{N}(1-\gamma_{\phi^{2}}{\varepsilon})+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,,\qquad\gamma_{\phi^{2}}=\frac{N+2}{N+8}\,,

(99)

\displaystyle\Delta_{T}

\displaystyle=2\Delta_{\phi}+\gamma_{T}{\varepsilon}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,,\qquad\;\;\lambda_{\phi\phi T}^{2}=2(1-\gamma_{T}{\varepsilon})+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,,\qquad\quad\,\gamma_{T}=\frac{2}{N+8}\,.

(100)

More generally we can construct singlet and symmetric-traceless operators of approximate twist two, that schematically read

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{\ell,0}\sim\partial_{\mu_{1}}\ldots\partial_{\mu_{\ell}}\phi^{2}_{a}\,,\qquad{\mathcal{O}}^{T}_{\ell,0}\sim\partial_{\mu_{1}}\ldots\partial_{\mu_{\ell}}\left(\phi_{a}\phi_{b}-\frac{\delta_{ab}}{N}\phi_{c}^{2}\right)\,.

(101)

These operators correspond to higher-spin currents in the free theory.
At the order we work, their dimension remains protected and their CFT data reads as in free theory:

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{\ell,0}}=2{\Delta_{\phi}}+\ell+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,,\qquad\lambda_{\phi\phi{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{\ell,0}}^{2}=\frac{2^{\ell+1}({\Delta_{\phi}})_{\ell}^{2}}{N\ell!(2{\Delta_{\phi}}+\ell-1)_{\ell}}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,,

(102)

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\mathcal{O}}^{T}_{\ell,0}}=2{\Delta_{\phi}}+\ell+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,,\qquad\lambda_{\phi\phi{\mathcal{O}}^{T}_{\ell,0}}^{2}=\frac{2^{\ell+1}({\Delta_{\phi}})_{\ell}^{2}}{\ell!(2{\Delta_{\phi}}+\ell-1)_{\ell}}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,.

(103)

We can also construct higher-twist families, for example at
n=1

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{\ell,1}\;\sim\;\partial^{2}\partial_{\mu_{1}}\ldots\partial_{\mu_{\ell}}\phi^{2}_{a}\,,\;\;\partial_{\mu_{1}}\ldots\partial_{\mu_{\ell}}(\phi^{2}_{a})^{2}\,.

(104)

Here we wrote two schematic operators with the same quantum numbers.
This follows because
\phi
has dimension one in the free theory limit, so the Laplacian
\partial^{2}
has the same dimension as
\phi_{a}^{2}
.
All in all, at each spin there can be multiple inequivalent operators
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
with nearly degenerate scaling dimensions.
In an OPE decomposition one can only extract the sum over this nearly degenerate contributions, which we denote by
\langle\!\langle\lambda_{\phi\phi{\mathcal{O}}}^{2}\rangle\!\rangle=\sum_{i}\lambda_{\phi\phi{\mathcal{O}}_{i}}^{2}
.
For the case of present interest, the OPE coefficients were found in
[
48
,
50
]

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\lambda_{\phi\phi{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{\ell,1}}^{2}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{\ell}\Gamma(\ell+2)^{2}}{\Gamma(2\ell+3)}\,\frac{(N+2)\left(\ell^{2}+3\ell+8\right)}{4N(N+8)^{2}(\ell+1)(\ell+2)}\,{\varepsilon}^{2}+O({\varepsilon}^{3})\,,

(105)

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\lambda_{\phi\phi{\mathcal{O}}^{T}_{\ell,1}}^{2}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{\ell}\Gamma(\ell+2)^{2}}{\Gamma(2\ell+3)}\,\frac{(N+2)\left(\ell^{2}+3\ell+8\right)-4N}{4N(N+8)^{2}(\ell+1)(\ell+2)}\,{\varepsilon}^{2}+O({\varepsilon}^{3})\,.

(106)

Finally, let us mention that higher-twist families are suppressed in
{\varepsilon}
, as was first observed in
[
22
]
, and later proved with analytic bootstrap in
[
48
,
50
]
:

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\lambda_{\phi\phi{\mathcal{O}}^{S/T}_{\ell,n\geq 2}}^{2}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle=O({\varepsilon}^{3})\,.

(107)

Furthermore, below we need the coupling between
\phi^{2}
and the leading singlet trajectory
[
44
]
:

\displaystyle\lambda_{\phi^{2}\phi^{2}\phi^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{N}}\left(1-\frac{3(N+2)}{2(N+8)}\,{\varepsilon}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\right)\,,

(108)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\phi^{2}\phi^{2}{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,0}^{S}}

\displaystyle=2\lambda_{\phi\phi{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,0}^{S}}\left(1+\frac{N+2}{N+8}\left(H_{\ell}-1\right){\varepsilon}+O({\varepsilon})\right)\,.

(109)

Turning our attention to the
\phi\times\phi^{2}
OPE, we find operators
{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,n}^{V}
that are vectors under
O(N)
.
These operators are highly degenerate, and even the leading family has mixing.
For this reason, we restrict our attention to the Ising model
N=1
, where the situation improves.
Let us look at triple-twist operators

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}^{-}_{\ell,0,i}\sim\partial_{\mu_{1}}\ldots\partial_{\mu_{\ell}}\phi^{3}\,,

(110)

where
i=1,...,d_{\ell}
distinguishes the
d_{\ell}
degenerate operators, and
{\mathcal{O}}^{-}
indicates these are odd under
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
It was shown in
[
49
]
that at each spin only one degenerate operator acquires anomalous dimension up to
O({\varepsilon}^{2})
corrections.
Furthermore, this operator is the only that has non-vanishing OPE coefficient
[
44
]
at the same order.
We call this operator
{\mathcal{O}}^{-}_{\ell,0}\equiv{\mathcal{O}}^{-}_{\ell,0,1}
, and restrict our attention to it.
Its CFT data reads
[
44
]

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\mathcal{O}}^{-}_{\ell,0}}

\displaystyle={\Delta_{\phi}}+\Delta_{\phi^{2}}+\frac{2}{3}\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{\ell+1}\,{\varepsilon}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,,

(111)

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}_{\phi\phi^{2}{\mathcal{O}}^{-}_{\ell,0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{\ell+1}\Gamma(\ell+2)^{2}(\ell+1+2(-1)^{\ell})}{\Gamma(2\ell+3)}+O({\varepsilon})\,.

(112)

The fact that
{\mathcal{O}}^{-}_{\ell,0,i\geq 2}
have subleading OPE coefficients and anomalous dimensions is what allowed us to treat the triple-twist family as non-degenerate in the bootstrap analysis of section
2
.
For the
O(N)
model for
N>1
this is no longer the case, so the bootstrap calculation would be more complicated, and also we cannot extract unmixed CFT data for these operators below.

4.2
Bulk CFT data

4.2.1
Singlet channel

Let us start presenting the CFT data in the
O(N)
-singlet sector, which is probed by the
F_{\phi\phi}
and
F_{\phi^{2}\phi^{2}}
correlators.
Remember that
F_{\phi\phi}
decomposes into two symmetry channels (
51
), the singlet one being

\displaystyle F_{S}(r,w)=\xi^{-{\Delta_{\phi}}}+\frac{a_{\phi}^{2}}{N}+\frac{N+2}{4N}\,{\varepsilon}\,H(r,w)+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,.

(113)

We can decompose this correlator in conformal blocks, and the result captures contributions of twist-two and twist-four families

\displaystyle\xi^{\Delta_{\phi}}F_{S}(r,w)

\displaystyle=1+\lambda_{\phi\phi\phi^{2}}a_{\phi^{2}}f_{\Delta_{\phi^{2}},0}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=2\\
\ell\text{ even}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}2^{-\ell}\lambda_{\phi\phi{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,0}^{S}}a_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,0}^{S}}f_{2\Delta_{\phi}+\ell,\ell}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\ell\text{ even}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}2^{-\ell}\langle\!\langle\lambda_{\phi\phi{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,1}^{S}}a_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,1}^{S}}\rangle\!\rangle f_{2\Delta_{\phi}+\ell+2,\ell}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,.

(114)

The precise values of the CFT data can be obtained from (
25
) and (
170
).
In particular, it can be checked that the expansion reproduces
a_{\phi^{2}}
as reported in (
77
).
Similarly, we can extract the one-point functions of the twist-two family, which are new predictions

\displaystyle a_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,0}^{S}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(N+8)}{\sqrt{N}}\frac{(\ell!)^{3}}{2^{\frac{3\ell+5}{2}}\left(\frac{\ell}{2}!\right)^{4}\sqrt{(2\ell)!}}\bigg[1

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\left(\frac{N^{2}-3N-22}{(N+8)^{2}}-2H_{\frac{\ell-1}{2}}+H_{\ell-\frac{1}{2}}+2H_{\ell}-H_{2\ell}\right)+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\bigg]\,.

(115)

Moving to twist four presents the complication of mixing between nearly-degenerate operators, so we can only extract the average CFT data:

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle a_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,1}^{S}}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\langle\!\langle\lambda_{\phi\phi{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,1}^{S}}a_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,1}^{S}}\rangle\!\rangle}{\langle\!\langle\lambda^{2}_{\phi\phi{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,1}^{S}}\rangle\!\rangle^{1/2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{-\frac{3\ell+6}{2}}((\ell+1)!)^{3}}{\left(\frac{\ell}{2}!\right)^{4}\sqrt{(2\ell+2)!}}\frac{(N+8)^{2}}{\sqrt{N(N+2)}\sqrt{(\ell+1)(\ell+2)(\ell^{2}+3\ell+8)}}+O({\varepsilon})\,,

(116)

For non-degenerate operators, the average has to agree with the actual value of
a_{\mathcal{O}}
. For instance, the operator
{\mathcal{O}}_{0,1}^{S}=\phi^{4}
is non-degenerate, and indeed we find agreement with (
82
).

The singlet channel is also probed by the
F_{\phi^{2}\phi^{2}}
correlation function.
In this case, we gain access to more high-twist families, although these are in general degenerate:
14
14

14

The notation
\langle\!\langle\lambda_{\mathcal{O}}a_{\mathcal{O}}f_{\Delta_{\mathcal{O}},\ell}\rangle\!\rangle
expresses the fact that we can extract
\langle\!\langle\lambda_{\mathcal{O}}a_{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\!\rangle
and
\langle\!\langle\lambda_{\mathcal{O}}a_{\mathcal{O}}\gamma_{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\!\rangle
, so the anomalous dimension is mixed with the OPE coefficients.

\displaystyle\xi^{{\Delta_{\phi^{2}}}}F_{\phi^{2}\phi^{2}}(r,w)

\displaystyle=1+\lambda_{\phi^{2}\phi^{2}\phi^{2}}a_{\phi^{2}}f_{\Delta_{\phi^{2}},0}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=2\\
\ell\text{ even}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}2^{-\ell}\lambda_{\phi^{2}\phi^{2}{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,0}^{S}}a_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,0}^{S}}f_{2\Delta_{\phi}+\ell,\ell}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\ell\text{ even}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}2^{-\ell}\langle\!\langle\lambda_{\phi^{2}\phi^{2}{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,n}^{S}}a_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,n}^{S}}f_{\Delta_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,n}^{S}},\ell}\rangle\!\rangle+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,.

(117)

We have checked that the CFT data of
\phi^{2}
and the twist-two family agree with (
115
).
It is not hard to extract the averaged CFT data from the formulas in appendix
C
, but the results are not particularly illuminating.
Instead, we content ourselves by mentioning that we checked once again that the one-point function
a_{\phi^{4}}
is correctly reproduced by our results.

4.2.2
Symmetric-traceless channel

We can similarly analyse symmetric-traceless operators.
In this case, the relevant part of
F_{\phi\phi}
is

\displaystyle F_{T}(r,w)=a_{\phi}^{2}+\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\,H(r,w)\,.

(118)

The structure of the block expansion is identical to the singlet channel, except the bulk identity is not present:

\displaystyle\xi^{\Delta_{\phi}}F_{T}(r,w)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\phi\phi T}a_{T}f_{\Delta_{T},0}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=2\\
\ell\text{ even}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}2^{-\ell}\lambda_{\phi\phi{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,0}^{T}}a_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,0}^{T}}f_{2\Delta_{\phi}+\ell,\ell}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\ell\text{ even}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}2^{-\ell}\langle\!\langle\lambda_{\phi\phi{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,1}^{T}}a_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,1}^{T}}\rangle\!\rangle f_{2\Delta_{\phi}+\ell+2,\ell}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,.

(119)

We have checked that this expansion leads to the correct value of
a_{T}
in (
78
).
The one-point functions of the twist-two family are again new predictions:

\displaystyle a_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,0}^{T}}=a_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,0}^{S}}\sqrt{N}\,.

(120)

Finally, we have degenerate twist-four operators, that have the following averaged CFT data

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle a_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,1}^{T}}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{-\frac{3\ell+6}{2}}((\ell+1)!)^{3}}{\left(\frac{\ell}{2}!\right)^{4}\sqrt{(2\ell+2)!}}\frac{(N+8)^{2}}{\sqrt{(\ell+1)(\ell+2)[(\ell^{2}+3\ell+8)(N+2)-4N]}}+O({\varepsilon})\,.

(121)

Once again, the scalar operator
\ell=0
agrees with the prediction for
a_{\phi^{2}T}
in (
82
).

4.2.3
Vector channel

Lastly, we consider the CFT data of vector operators under
O(N)
, which can be accessed from
F_{\phi\phi^{2}}
:

\displaystyle\xi^{\frac{1}{2}(\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\phi^{2}})}F_{\phi\phi^{2}}(r,w)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\phi\phi\phi^{2}}a_{\phi}f_{{\Delta_{\phi}},0}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=2\\
\ell\text{ even}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}2^{-\ell}\langle\!\langle\lambda_{\phi\phi^{2}{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,0}^{V}}a_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,0}^{V}}f_{\Delta_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,0}^{V}},\ell}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\ell\text{ even}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}2^{-\ell}\langle\!\langle\lambda_{\phi\phi{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,n}^{V}}a_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,n}^{V}}\rangle\!\rangle f_{2\Delta_{\phi}+\ell+2n,\ell}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,.

(122)

First, we checked that the operator
\phi
appears with the correct one-point function given in (
73
).
Second, we checked that the scalar in the leading-twist family, which should be identified as
{\mathcal{O}}_{0,0}^{V}\sim\phi^{2}\phi_{a}
, decouples from the expansion.
This is expected because of multiplet recombination, since
\partial^{2}\phi\propto{\varepsilon}\phi^{2}\phi_{a}
in the interacting theory, so
\phi^{2}\phi_{a}
becomes a conformal descendant.
However, we show in appendix
C
that this expansion has to be done with great care.

Finally, we remind that the triple-twist family is degenerate
[
49
]
.
When
N=1
we can ignore this degeneracy at tree-level, as discussed below equation (
110
), and we extract

\displaystyle a_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell,0}^{-}}=-\frac{27\,\Gamma(2\ell+3)^{1/2}}{2^{\frac{3\ell}{2}+4}\sqrt{\ell+3}\,\Gamma(\ell+2)}+O({\varepsilon})\,.

(123)

With the methods in appendix
C
, we can also extract the
O({\varepsilon})
correction, but it is not guaranteed it corresponds to unmixed CFT data, so we ignore it here.
Similarly, with the help of the appendix it is possible to extract averaged CFT data for
N>1
and for the higher-twist families
n\geq 1
.

4.3
Defect CFT data

We now move on to the defect conformal block expansion.
In this case, the correlators probe operators
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{s,n}^{S}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{s,n}^{V}
that are singlet or vector under the
O(N-1)
symmetry preserved by the defect.
These defect operators have tree-level dimension

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\mathcal{O}}_{s,n}^{S}}\approx{\Delta_{\phi}}+s+n\,,\qquad\Delta_{{\mathcal{O}}_{s,n}^{V}}\approx{\Delta_{\phi}}+s+n\,.

(124)

As in the bulk, there is a large degeneracy of defect operators in each sector, except for the leading trajectory which is non-degenerate

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{s,0}^{S}\sim\partial_{i_{1}}\ldots\partial_{i_{s}}\phi_{1}\,,\qquad\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{s,0}^{V}\sim\partial_{i_{1}}\ldots\partial_{i_{s}}\phi_{\hat{a}}\,.

(125)

Above
i=1,\ldots,d-1
labels directions orthogonal to the defect and
\hat{a}=2,\ldots,N
labels
O(N-1)
indices.
It is easy to see that already at
n=1
there is at least two-fold degeneracy, namely

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{s,1}^{S}\;\sim\;\partial_{i_{1}}\ldots\partial_{i_{s}}\phi^{2}_{1}\,,\;\;\partial_{i_{1}}\ldots\partial_{i_{s}}\phi^{2}_{\hat{a}}\,,\;\ldots

(126)

The mixing problem for
n=1
and
s=0
has been solved in
[
14
,
19
]
using diagrammatic techniques.
Below we shall not attempt to solve mixing problems, and instead the focus is on the leading-twist family.

4.3.1
Singlet channel

Let us start analyzing singlet operators under
O(N-1)
, which can be accessed from the three correlators at our disposal.
Considering
F_{\phi\phi}
, we need to take the contribution from the correct tensor structure (
55
):

\displaystyle F_{\widehat{S}}(r,w)=a_{\phi}^{2}+\xi^{-{\Delta_{\phi}}}+\frac{3{\varepsilon}}{4}\,H(r,w)\,.

(127)

We then perform the defect conformal block expansion, and observe that at this order in
{\varepsilon}
only the leading-twist trajectory appears

\displaystyle F_{\widehat{S}}(r,w)=a_{\phi}^{2}+\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}2^{-s}b^{2}_{\phi\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{s,0}}\widehat{f}_{\widehat{\Delta}_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{s,0}},s}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,.

(128)

The algorithm to obtain the OPE coefficients is detailed in appendix
C.2
, and here we simply report the CFT data:
15
15

15

When this work was being prepared for publication, reference
[
20
]
appeared. The anomalous dimensions in (
129
) and (
137
) agree perfectly with the results in their appendix C.

\displaystyle\widehat{\Delta}_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{s,0}}=1+s-\frac{s-1}{2s+1}{\varepsilon}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,,

(129)

\displaystyle b_{\phi\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{s,0}}=2^{s/2}\left(1-\frac{3{\varepsilon}}{2(2s+1)^{2}}-\frac{2(s-1)H_{s}+3H_{s-\frac{1}{2}}}{4(2s+1)}\,{\varepsilon}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\right)\,.

(130)

We can perform several consistency checks on this result.
First, focusing on
s=0
we recover the correct dimension of the operator
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{0,0}=\widehat{\phi}
, as well as
b_{\phi\widehat{\phi}}
computed in (
88
).
Second, we consider the displacement operator
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{1,0}=\vec{D}\sim\vec{\partial}\phi
, for which the scaling dimension agrees with the Ward identity prediction
\widehat{\Delta}_{D}=2
.

We can also access the singlet channel using the mixed two-point function between
\phi_{a}
and
\phi^{2}
given in (
95
).
In this case, both the leading-twist and higher even-twist families appear

\displaystyle F_{\phi\phi^{2}}(r,w)

\displaystyle=a_{\phi}a_{\phi^{2}}+\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}2^{-s}b_{\phi\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{s,0}}b_{\phi^{2}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{s,0}}\widehat{f}_{\widehat{\Delta}_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{s,0}},s}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}2^{-s}\langle\!\langle b_{\phi\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{s,2n+1}}b_{\phi^{2}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{s,2n+1}}\rangle\!\rangle\widehat{f}_{2+s+2n,s}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,.

(131)

For the leading-twist family, we obtain once again the scaling dimensions in (
129
).
Regarding OPE coefficients, we use (
130
) to disentangle their product:

\displaystyle b_{\phi^{2}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{s,0}}

\displaystyle=-2^{\frac{s-1}{2}}\sqrt{\frac{N+8}{N}}\Bigg(1+\frac{N-4}{4(N+8)}\left(H_{s}-2\log 2\right){\varepsilon}+\frac{3H_{s}-3H_{s-\frac{1}{2}}}{4(2s+1)}\,{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\hskip 120.00018pt-\frac{N^{2}+23N+54}{4(N+8)^{2}}\,{\varepsilon}-\frac{3{\varepsilon}}{2(2s+1)^{2}}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\Bigg)\,.

(132)

Here we have performed yet another consistency check, by seeing that the Ward identity (
40
) is satisfied for arbitrary
N
.
As a curiosity, note that the even-twist CFT data admits a simple closed form expression:

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle b_{\phi\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{s,2n+1}}b_{\phi^{2}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{s,2n+1}}\rangle\!\rangle=-\frac{N+2}{\sqrt{N(N+8)}}\frac{2^{\frac{2s-1}{2}}(s+1)_{2m+1}}{(2m+1)(m+s+1)\left(s+\frac{3}{2}\right)_{2m}}\,.

(133)

Finally we look at the two-point function of
\phi^{2}
, which admits the expansion

\displaystyle F_{\phi^{2}\phi^{2}}(r,w)=a_{\phi^{2}}^{2}+\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}2^{-s}\,b_{\phi^{2}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{s,0}}^{2}\widehat{f}_{\widehat{\Delta}_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{s,0}},s}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}2^{-s}\,\langle\!\langle b_{\phi^{2}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{s,n}}^{2}\widehat{f}_{\widehat{\Delta}_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{S}_{s,n}},s}\rangle\!\rangle+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,.

(134)

We have checked that the leading-twist expansion agrees with (
129
) and (
132
).
The CFT data for higher-twists follows from the methods of appendix
C.2
, but the result is not particularly illuminating.

4.3.2
Vector channel

Finally, we turn our attention to operators which are vector under
O(N-1)
, which can only be obtained from the two-point function of
\phi_{a}
in (
92
):

\displaystyle F_{\widehat{V}}(r,w)=\xi^{-{\Delta_{\phi}}}+\frac{{\varepsilon}}{4}\,H(r,w)+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,.

(135)

The structure of the CFT data is similar as for
F_{\widehat{S}}
, with the only difference that the defect identity is not present (as expected):

\displaystyle F_{\widehat{V}}(r,w)=\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}2^{-s}b^{2}_{\phi\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{V}_{s,0}}\widehat{f}_{\widehat{\Delta}_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{V}_{s,0}},s}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,.

(136)

Using the methods from the appendix we extract the CFT data

\displaystyle\widehat{\Delta}_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{V}_{s,0}}=1+s-\frac{s}{2s+1}{\varepsilon}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,,

(137)

\displaystyle b_{\phi\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{V}_{s,0}}=2^{s/2}\left(1-\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2(2s+1)^{2}}-\frac{2sH_{s}+H_{s-\frac{1}{2}}}{4(2s+1)}\,{\varepsilon}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\right)\,.

(138)

The tilt operator
t
corresponds to
s=0
, and indeed we recover the correct dimension
\widehat{\Delta}_{t}=1
and the correct coefficient
b_{\phi t}
in (
88
).

5
Conclusions

The main result of this paper are the mixed two-point functions of the order parameter
\phi
and energy operator
\phi^{2}
in the presence of a localized magnetic field line defect
[
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
]
, see equations (
92
), (
95
) and (
97
).
We started using analytic bootstrap for these mixed correlators, focusing mostly on the
N=1
Wilson-Fisher fixed point.
Having access to mixed correlators allowed us to impose multiple consistency conditions that fixed uniquely the final result.
For
N>1
we bootstrapped a single correlator, and although the result has one unfixed parameter, we believe the extension to mixed correlators would also fix this parameter.
We have supplemented the bootstrap analysis with perturbative calculations using Feynman diagrams, finding perfect agreement when comparison was possible.
Finally, we performed a detailed OPE analysis that provides new predictions for CFT data that might be useful for future studies.

This work demonstrates that the bootstrap is a powerful method to study conformal defects.
Although our analysis relies on the
{\varepsilon}
–expansion, the analytic techniques we used hold non-perturbatively, so the natural next step is to go beyond pertubation theory.
One idea is to consider truncated crossing equations
[
56
,
57
]
, an approach that works well for boundary CFT
[
28
,
16
]
.
Although preliminary results
[
58
]
are still somewhat far from perturbative estimates
[
14
]
and Monte Carlo simulations
[
13
,
12
]
, perhaps the method of
[
59
]
to include more operators in the truncation can help.
An alternative is to use analytic bootstrap technology, similar in spirit to
[
60
,
61
,
62
,
63
,
64
]
, but in a setup with a line defect.
Note that defect CFT kinematics are somewhat similar to CFT at finite temperature, where this approach was successful
[
65
]
.
To pursue either of these directions it will be important to remember the lessons from the present work.
Namely, that to uniquely identify the localized magnetic line defect it is a good idea to bootstrap mixed correlators and to impose the Ward identities satisfied by the displacement operator.

If we allow ourselves to remain in the perturbative regime, there are many models that can be studied with similar techniques to ours.
One natural candidate is the “magnetic Wilson line”
[
66
,
67
]
recently revisited in
[
45
]
, for which we are planning a more detailed
{\varepsilon}
–expansion analysis.
Similarly, the study of bulk two-point functions might provide useful information about line defects in the Gross-Neveau model
[
68
,
20
]
or in melonic CFTs
[
46
]
.
In the context of supersymmetric theories with a holographic description, there are many interesting defects which are engineered by intersecting branes in the string dual.
The example of a Maldacena-Wilson line in
{\mathcal{N}}=4
SYM at strong coupling has been studied with bootstrap techniques in
[
41
,
30
]
.
In principle, these results can be used to obtain the leading stringy corrections to the effective action of the dual branes
[
69
]
.
16
16

16

I thank Victor Rodriguez and Yifan Wang for useful discussions on this point.

Similar remarks also apply to M2-brane defects in
6d

(2,0)
theory, as well as the half-BPS line defect in
3d

{\mathcal{N}}=8
ABJM theory.

Finally, it would also be interesting to clarify several technical aspects of our calculation.
The first is the assumption about Regge behavior, namely that the correlator is constant as
|w|\to\infty
.
At the moment we lack a non-perturbative bound on Regge behavior for defect CFT, except for the case of boundary CFT
[
70
,
71
]
, and it would be interesting to see if such a bound could be derived.
Relatedly, from the AdS/DCFT perspective
[
70
,
72
,
71
]
one can add an arbitrary number of Witten diagrams to a correlator while preserving crossing, but worsening Regge behavior as much as desired.
It would be interesting to understand why in theories of interest these contributions seem to be absent.
One last technical problem we should mention are the families of nearly-degenerate operators, which pose the main obstacle to pushing analytic bootstrap to higher perturbative orders.
It would be fascinating if the bootstrap of higher-point correlators
[
73
,
74
]
gives a handle on these mixing problems.

Appendix A
Review of defect CFT

A.1
Kinematics

In defect conformal field theory (DCFT) one considers two theories, the bulk and the defect CFTs, which interact in a non-trivial fashion.
On the one hand, the physics far away from the defect is governed by the bulk CFT, which is defined by an infinite set of local operators
{\mathcal{O}}
that satisfy unitarity and crossing symmetry.
As usual, we unit-normalize two-point functions such that the normalization of the three-point function contains dynamical information:
17
17

17

In this work, only symmetric-traceless operators play a role, and it is convenient to employ index-free notation
{\mathcal{O}}(x,\eta)={\mathcal{O}}(x)^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{\ell}}\eta_{\mu_{1}}\ldots\eta_{\mu_{\ell}}
where
\eta\cdot\eta=0
. Below we also introduce
\vec{w}
with
\vec{w}^{2}=0
to denote polarization under transverse rotations.

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{1}(x_{1},\eta_{1}){\mathcal{O}}_{2}(x_{2},\eta_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta_{{\mathcal{O}}_{1},{\mathcal{O}}_{2}}}{x_{12}^{2\Delta_{1}}}\left(\eta_{1}\cdot\eta_{2}-\frac{2\,\eta_{1}\cdot x_{12}\,\eta_{2}\cdot x_{12}}{x_{12}^{2}}\right)^{\ell}\,,

(139)

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{1}(x_{1}){\mathcal{O}}_{2}(x_{2}){\mathcal{O}}_{3}(x_{3},\eta)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{{\mathcal{O}}_{1}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}{\mathcal{O}}_{3}}}{x_{12}^{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{3}+\ell}x_{13}^{\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{12}-\ell}x_{23}^{\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{12}-\ell}}\left(\frac{\eta\cdot x_{13}}{x_{13}^{2}}-\frac{\eta\cdot x_{23}}{x_{23}^{2}}\right)^{\ell}\,.

(140)

Here and in what follows
\Delta_{12}=\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}
.
The CFT data
\{\Delta_{i},\lambda_{ijk}\}
fully determines the bulk theory.
This data has to do with local (short-distance) physics, and it is not modified by the presence of a defect.

On the other hand, there is a CFT that lives in the
p
-dimensional worldvolume of the defect.
The defect theory is consistent on its own, and in particular it is defined by a set of defect local operators
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}
and the corresponding CFT data
\{\widehat{\Delta}_{i},\widehat{\lambda}_{ijk}\}
, which is constrained by unitarity and crossing.
Defect operators are labeled by
(\widehat{\Delta},j,s)
, the quantum numbers of the symmetry group preserved by the conformal defect
SO(p+1,1)\oplus SO(q)
.
To be more precise,
\widehat{\Delta}
,
j
are the
p
-dimensional scaling dimension and parallel spin, whereas
s
is the transverse spin under
SO(q)
rotations.

The structure of defect CFT is particularly interesting when the bulk and defect interact.
In this case, the defect theory does not admit a local stress tensor, because energy can be exchanged between defect and bulk.
The role of the stress-tensor is played by a defect operator called displacement operator, while the role of a conserved current is played by the tilt operator whenever the defect breaks a continuous global symmetry.
As we discuss in more detail in the main text, the displacement and tilt have fixed dimension
\widehat{\Delta}_{D}=p+1
and
\widehat{\Delta}_{t}=p
, and satisfy Ward identities that relate certain OPE coefficients
[
23
,
16
]
.

In the present paper, our focus is on observables that involve the coupling between the bulk and defect theories.
The simplest such observable are bulk one-point functions, which are zero in the absence of a defect, but appear due to the partially-broken conformal symmetry:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle\!\langle{\mathcal{O}}(x,\eta)\rangle\!\rangle&=\frac{a_{\mathcal{O}}}{|\vec{x}|^{\Delta}}\left(\frac{(\vec{\eta}\cdot\vec{x})^{2}}{\vec{x}\cdot\vec{x}}-\vec{\eta}\cdot\vec{\eta}\right)^{\ell/2}\,.\end{split}

(141)

Here and below the double-bracket notation
\langle\!\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle\!\rangle
indicates that an expectation value is taken in the presence of a defect.
Furthermore, we split vectors
x=(\hat{x},\vec{x})
into parallel coordinates
\hat{x}
and orthogonal coordinates
\vec{x}
.
Since bulk operators are unit normalized far away from the defect (
139
), the one-point function coefficient
a_{\mathcal{O}}
contains dynamical information about the DCFT.
Note that (
141
) only makes sense for even spin
\ell
, and odd-spin operators have vanishing one-point functions.
18
18

18

An important exception are parity-breaking defects in codimension two, see
[
40
]
for a thorough discussion.

More generally, given a bulk and a defect operator, their two-point function is kinematically fixed and the normalization
b_{{\mathcal{O}}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}
contains dynamical information.
The simplest example is a bulk scalar coupled to an operator with transverse spin
s
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle\!\langle{\mathcal{O}}(x_{1})\widehat{\mathcal{O}}(\hat{x}_{2},\vec{w})\rangle\!\rangle&=b_{{\mathcal{O}}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}\frac{(\vec{x}_{1}\cdot\vec{w})^{s}}{(\vec{x}_{1}^{2}+\hat{x}_{12}^{2})^{\widehat{\Delta}}|\vec{x}_{1}|^{\Delta-\widehat{\Delta}+s}}\,.\end{split}

(142)

In fact, the CFT data associated to the defect
\{\widehat{\Delta}_{i},\widehat{\lambda}_{ijk},a_{i},b_{ij}\}
is complete, in the sense that any correlation function can in principle be reconstructed from it using operator product expansions.

The bootstrap philosophy is somewhat reversed compared to this discussion.
The idea is to consider higher-point functions, which contain infinite amount of CFT data through the OPE, and to constrain
\{\widehat{\Delta}_{i},\widehat{\lambda}_{ijk},a_{i},b_{ij}\}
demanding compatibility of the different OPE channels.
In a favorable situation, such as the one described in section
2
, one can completely fix unknown CFT data, typically in a perturbative regime.

There have been mostly two approaches in analytic studies of defects.
The first, which we do not pursue here, focuses mostly on the theory that lives on the defect, see for example work on monodromy defects
[
24
,
75
,
19
]
or holographic defects at strong coupling
[
76
,
77
,
78
,
79
,
80
,
81
]
.
From the bootstrap perspective, one can consider crossing of defect four-point functions, which enjoys positivity but unfortunately it is hard to leverage known information from the bulk.
Alternatively, our approach is to consider two-point functions of bulk operators in the presence of the defect, see for example
[
22
,
82
,
29
,
43
,
54
,
40
,
41
]
.
The two-point crossing equation does not have positivity, but on the flip side it allows one to extract information from the bulk to understand the defect dynamics.
The two-point functions are useful observables, because by means of operator product expansions, they capture dynamical information of infinite families of operators in the bulk and in the defect.

A.2
Bulk two-point functions

In the present paper we focus on the simplest defect setup that allows for the bootstrap program.
These are two-point function of bulk scalar operators
19
19

19

Another interesting possibility is to consider external operators that are not scalars, see for instance
[
26
,
83
]
.

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{1}(x_{1}){\mathcal{O}}_{2}(x_{2})\rangle\!\rangle=\frac{F(r,w)}{|\vec{x}_{1}|^{\Delta_{1}}|\vec{x}_{2}|^{\Delta_{2}}}\,.

(143)

Here we use the cross-ratios
r
,
w
introduced in
[
25
]
, but with the notation of
[
32
]
:

\displaystyle r+\frac{1}{r}=\frac{\hat{x}_{12}^{2}+\vec{x}_{1}^{\,2}+\vec{x}_{2}^{\,2}}{|\vec{x}_{1}||\vec{x}_{2}|}\,,\qquad w+\frac{1}{w}=\frac{2\,\vec{x}_{1}\cdot\vec{x}_{2}}{|\vec{x}_{1}||\vec{x}_{2}|}\,.

(144)

In certain cases, it is more convenient to work with

\displaystyle z=rw\,,\qquad{\bar{z}}=\frac{r}{w}\,,

(145)

which have a similar interpretation to the
z,{\bar{z}}
cross-ratios of four-point functions.
In Euclidean signature
z,{\bar{z}}
are complex coordinates on a plane orthogonal to the defect, so
r
corresponds to the radial coordinate and
w
to a phase.
In Lorentzian signature
z,{\bar{z}}
are real and independent.
It is also convenient to introduce the variable
\xi
:

\displaystyle\xi=\frac{(1-rw)(w-r)}{rw}=\frac{x_{12}^{2}}{|\vec{x}_{1}||\vec{x}_{2}|}\,.

(146)

After these preliminary remarks, let us consider the two possible OPE expansions of the correlator
F(r,w)
.
The first possibility is to expand the bulk operator in defect modes, schematically as
{\mathcal{O}}\sim\sum_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}b_{{\mathcal{O}}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}
.
For a concrete example, in the localized magnetic line defect the first terms in the defect expansion of the order parameter
\phi
read

\displaystyle\lim_{|\vec{x}|\to 0}\phi(x)\sim\frac{a_{\phi}}{|\vec{x}|^{{\Delta_{\phi}}}}+\frac{b_{\phi\widehat{\phi}}}{|\vec{x}|^{{\Delta_{\phi}}-\widehat{\Delta}_{\widehat{\phi}}}}\,\widehat{\phi}+\frac{b_{\phi D}}{|\vec{x}|^{{\Delta_{\phi}}-\widehat{\Delta}_{D}+1}}\,\vec{x}\cdot\vec{D}+\ldots\,.

(147)

The first term is the contribution of the defect identity
a_{\phi}=b_{\phi\widehat{\mathds{1}}}
, while the third term is the contribution of the displacement operator.

As usual, the defect expansion can be organized into conformal primaries and descendants, where the contribution of descendants can be resummed.
When applied to the two-point function (
143
), this leads to the defect-channel conformal block decomposition

\displaystyle F(r,w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}2^{-s}\,b_{{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}\,b_{{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}\,\widehat{f}_{{\widehat{\Delta}},s}(r,w)\,,

(148)

where the sum runs over defect primaries, and the blocks capture contributions from all descendants.
The somewhat awkward factor
2^{-s}
ensures that, with the definition of
b_{{\mathcal{O}}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}
in (
142
), the blocks enjoy a nice normalization
[
23
]
:

\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{{\widehat{\Delta}},s}(r,w)=r^{\widehat{\Delta}}\,_{2}F_{1}\left(\frac{p}{2},\widehat{\Delta};\widehat{\Delta}+1-\frac{p}{2};r^{2}\right)w^{-s}\,_{2}F_{1}\left(-s,\frac{q}{2}-1;2-\frac{q}{2}-s;w^{2}\right)\,.

(149)

The correlator
F(r,w)
admits a second expansion when the external operators approach each other.
This is the usual OPE limit
{\mathcal{O}}_{1}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\sim\sum_{{\mathcal{O}}}\lambda_{12{\mathcal{O}}}{\mathcal{O}}
, where again the expression is schematic.
When applied to the two-point function, the OPE leads to the conformal block decomposition

\displaystyle\xi^{\frac{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}}{2}}F(r,w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathcal{O}},\,\ell\text{ even}}2^{-\ell}\,\lambda_{{\mathcal{O}}_{1}{\mathcal{O}}_{2}{\mathcal{O}}}\,a_{{\mathcal{O}}}\,f^{\Delta_{12}}_{\Delta_{\mathcal{O}},\ell_{\mathcal{O}}}(r,w)\,.

(150)

The sum runs over even-spin operators because odd-spin operators have vanishing one-point function, see (
141
).
Here it was natural to multiply the left-hand side by
\xi^{\frac{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}}{2}}
because then the conformal blocks only depend on
\Delta_{12}
.
Whenever the external operators are equal, we drop the extra label and use
f_{\Delta,\ell}
to unclutter the notation.
Once again, the factor
2^{-\ell}
is compatible with the standard definitions (
140
) and (
141
), and what we think is the most natural normalization for the blocks.
Although they cannot be written as simple special functions, the conformal blocks are computable from the following infinite sum
[
27
,
84
]
:

\displaystyle f^{\Delta_{12}}_{\Delta,\ell}(r,w)=

\displaystyle\;r^{-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{12}}\sum_{m,n=0}^{\infty}\frac{4^{m-n}\left(\frac{-\ell}{2}\right)_{m}}{m!n!}

\displaystyle\frac{\left(\frac{\Delta-1}{2}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{1-\ell-p}{2}\right)_{m}\left(\frac{\Delta-p}{2}\right)_{n}\left(-\frac{\ell+\Delta+2+\Delta_{12}}{2}\right)_{m}\left(\frac{\ell+\Delta-\Delta_{12}}{2}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{\ell+\Delta+\Delta_{12}}{2}\right)_{n-m}}{\left(-\frac{d}{2}-\ell+2\right)_{m}\left(-\frac{d}{2}+\Delta+1\right)_{n}\left(-\frac{\ell+\Delta-3}{2}\right)_{m}\left(\frac{\ell+\Delta+1}{2}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{\ell+\Delta-1}{2}\right)_{n-m}}

\displaystyle\left(1-r^{2}\right)^{\ell-2m}{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\!\!\begin{array}[]{c c}\frac{\Delta+\ell-\Delta_{12}}{2}-m+n,\,\frac{\Delta+\ell}{2}-m+n\\
\Delta+\ell-2m+2n\end{array};1-r^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\left(\tfrac{(w-r)(1-rw)}{w}\right)^{\frac{\Delta-\ell}{2}+m+n}{}_{4}F_{3}\left(\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}-n,\,-m,\,\frac{\Delta_{12}+1}{2},\,\frac{\Delta_{12}+\Delta-\ell-d+2}{2}\\
\frac{\Delta_{12}-\Delta-\ell-2n+2}{2},\,\frac{\Delta_{12}+\Delta+\ell-2m}{2},\,\frac{\Delta-\ell-d+3}{2}\end{array};1\right)\,.

Whenever we compute conformal block decompositions, we also find it convenient to use the lightcone representation (
164
).

To summarize, the two-point function
F(r,w)
can be expanded in two different ways. When one operator approaches the defect we have
r\to 0
and it is natural to use the defect-channel decomposition (
148
).
On the other hand, when the two operators approach each other we have
\xi\to 0
and it is natural to use the bulk-channel decomposition (
150
).
The equivalence of these two expansions can be thought of as crossing equation, that can be used to solve theories of interest.
In this work we use two analytic tools to solve crossing, the dispersion relation (
4
), and the inversion formula that we now discuss.

A.3
Lorentzian inversion formula

The Lorentzian inversion formula is a tool for reconstructing the defect CFT data, which is encapsulated in a function
b({\widehat{\Delta}},s)
, starting from the single discontinuity of a correlator
\Disc F
.
The crucial property, emphasized in section
2.1
, is that the discontinuity is a simpler object than
F
, specially in perturbative settings.
For the application in section
2.2.2
, we have found it more convenient to work in
z,{\bar{z}}
coordinates, when the inversion formula reads
[
32
]

\displaystyle b({\widehat{\Delta}},s)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dz}{2z}z^{-\frac{{\widehat{\Delta}}-s}{2}}\int_{1}^{1/z}\frac{d{\bar{z}}}{2\pi i}(1-z\bar{z})(\bar{z}-z)\bar{z}^{-\frac{{\widehat{\Delta}}+s+4}{2}}

(155)

\displaystyle\quad\times{}_{2}F_{1}\left(1-{\widehat{\Delta}},1-\frac{p}{2},-{\widehat{\Delta}}+\frac{p}{2}+1,z{\bar{z}}\right){}_{2}F_{1}\left(s+1,2-\frac{q}{2},\frac{q}{2}+s,\frac{z}{{\bar{z}}}\right)\text{Disc}\,F(z,{\bar{z}})\,,

and the discontinuity is computed around the cut
{\bar{z}}\in[1,\infty)

\displaystyle\text{Disc}\,F(z,{\bar{z}})=F(z,{\bar{z}}+i0)-F(z,{\bar{z}}-i0),\qquad{\bar{z}}\geq 1\,.

(156)

Note that the discontinuities in terms of
r,w
and
z,{\bar{z}}
are negative of each other
\Disc_{\bar{z}}=-\Disc_{w}
, we hope this does not create confusion.

To recover the CFT data from
b({\widehat{\Delta}},s)
, observe that the location of the poles are scaling dimensions, while the residues are OPE coefficients:

b({\widehat{\Delta}},s)=-\sum_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}\frac{2^{-s}\,b_{{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}b_{{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}}{{\widehat{\Delta}}-\widehat{\Delta}_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}}\,.

(157)

Since we work in pertubation theory, we can split the dimensions into a tree-level and a perturbative correction
\widehat{\Delta}_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}=\widehat{\Delta}^{\text{tree}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}+\widehat{\gamma}_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}
.
In this case, the anomalous dimension comes from the double poles:

b({\widehat{\Delta}},s)=-\sum_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}2^{-s}\,b_{{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}\,b_{{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}\left(\frac{1}{{\widehat{\Delta}}-\widehat{\Delta}^{\text{tree}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}}+\frac{\widehat{\gamma}_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}}{\big({\widehat{\Delta}}-\widehat{\Delta}^{\text{tree}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{O}}}\big)^{2}}\right)\,.

(158)

In section
2.2.2
we extract the defect CFT data using this formalism, and then reconstruct the correlator summing the defect expansion (
148
).
As shown in
[
30
,
31
]
, this is completely equivalent to applying the integral transform
\DR[F(r,w)]
.

Appendix B
Discontinuity of
\phi^{2}

The purpose of this appendix is to compute the discontinuity of the bulk-channel conformal block corresponding to
\phi^{2}\equiv\phi_{a}\phi_{a}
.
We find it more convenient to use
z=rw
and
{\bar{z}}=r/w
coordinates.
Let us compute the discontinuity for a scalar block
\Delta=2{\Delta_{\phi}}+\gamma
, where for simplicity we take the external operators to be equal.
From the series representations of bulk blocks (
A.2
) or (
164
), we observe that removing the prefactor

\displaystyle f_{\Delta,0}(z,{\bar{z}})=\big[(1-z)(1-{\bar{z}})\big]^{\frac{\Delta}{2}}\tilde{f}_{\Delta,0}(z,{\bar{z}})\,,

(159)

the expansion of
\tilde{f}_{\Delta,0}(z,{\bar{z}})
is in integer powers of
(1-z),(1-{\bar{z}})
.
With this information, we compute the discontinuity assuming
\gamma
is small:

\displaystyle\Disc\xi^{-\frac{1}{2}{\Delta_{\phi}}}f_{\Delta,0}(z,{\bar{z}})

\displaystyle=(z{\bar{z}})^{\frac{1}{2}{\Delta_{\phi}}}\tilde{f}_{\Delta,0}(z,{\bar{z}})\Disc\!\big[(1-z)(1-{\bar{z}})\big]^{\frac{1}{2}\gamma}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{2}\gamma(z{\bar{z}})^{\frac{1}{2}{\Delta_{\phi}}}\tilde{f}_{\Delta,0}(z,{\bar{z}})\Disc\log(1-{\bar{z}})

\displaystyle=-i\pi\gamma(z{\bar{z}})^{\frac{1}{2}{\Delta_{\phi}}}\tilde{f}_{\Delta,0}(z,{\bar{z}})\,.

(160)

Remember that the discontinuity is computed around the cut
{\bar{z}}\in[1,+\infty)
, see (
156
).
In the second line we Taylor expanded to leading order in
\gamma
, which is the only term we need in this paper.
With similar manipulations, one can prove (
12
).

Let us now compute the discontinuity of
\phi^{2}
.
Note that the discontinuity (
160
) is order
{\varepsilon}
because
\gamma\propto{\varepsilon}
.
We can evaluate the result for
{\varepsilon}=0
, which simplifies the calculation, and all is left is to obtain
\tilde{f}_{2,0}
in
d=4
dimensions.
Using the series representation (
A.2
) we find

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{2,0}(z,{\bar{z}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\big[(1-z)(1-{\bar{z}})\big]^{n}}{4^{n}(2n+1)}\,{}_{2}F_{1}\left(n+1,n+1;2n+2;1-z{\bar{z}}\right)\,.

(161)

It is hard to compute this sum in full generality, but fortunately in section
2.2.2
we only need its expansion around
z=0
to all orders in
{\bar{z}}
.
This can be achieved by expanding the summand in (
161
) as
z\to 0
and then summing over
{\bar{z}}
.
For simplicity, let us first look at the leading-order term
z^{0}

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{2,0}(z,{\bar{z}})\big|_{z^{0}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma(n+\frac{1}{2})}{\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma(n+1)}(1-{\bar{z}})^{n}\big(2H_{n}+\log z{\bar{z}}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{{\bar{z}}}}\log\left(\frac{16{\bar{z}}^{2}}{(1+\sqrt{{\bar{z}}})^{4}}\right)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{{\bar{z}}}}\log z{\bar{z}}\,.

(162)

One can similarly expand (
161
) to higher orders in
z
and compute the
{\bar{z}}
sums order by order.
A useful trick is to notice that all sums are essentially of the form (
162
) after acting with certain
{\bar{z}}
derivatives.
This observation allows us to compute the expansion to high orders, and here we simply show the first few terms:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{f}_{2,0}(z,{\bar{z}})&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{{\bar{z}}}}\log\left(\frac{16{\bar{z}}}{z(1+\sqrt{{\bar{z}}})^{4}}\right)\left(1+z\frac{({\bar{z}}+1)^{2}}{4{\bar{z}}}+z^{2}\frac{\left(3{\bar{z}}^{2}+2{\bar{z}}+3\right)^{2}}{64{\bar{z}}^{2}}+\ldots\right)\\
&\quad-\frac{\left(\sqrt{{\bar{z}}}-1\right)^{2}}{2{\bar{z}}^{3/2}}\left(z({\bar{z}}+1)+z^{2}\frac{\left(7{\bar{z}}+2\sqrt{{\bar{z}}}+7\right)\left(3{\bar{z}}^{2}+2{\bar{z}}+3\right)}{32{\bar{z}}}+\ldots\right)\,.\end{split}

(163)

Restoring appropriate prefactors, this gives (
2.2.2
).

Appendix C
Block expansions

In this appendix we present the conformal block decomposition of the two-point functions (
92
), (
95
) and (
97
).
Here we consider each of the building blocks separately, and we present the combined CFT data in section
4
.

To obtain the conformal block decomposition it is convenient to work with
z=rw
and
{\bar{z}}=r/w
, because the expansion is
z,{\bar{z}}
naturally organizes by the twist.
For example, in the bulk channel we consider the lightcone limit
|1-z|\ll|1-{\bar{z}}|\ll 1
, and leading order in
1-z
and all orders in
1-{\bar{z}}
gives the CFT data of the leading-twist family.
To obtain the CFT data, we expand the correlator and the conformal blocks to high order in
1-{\bar{z}}
using
Mathematica
.
Solving for the unknown coefficients, it is often possible to guess a closed form expression.

C.1
Bulk channel

C.1.1
Lightcone blocks

We compute the bulk-channel expansions in the limit
|1-z|\ll|1-{\bar{z}}|\ll 1
.
Since we organize our calculations twist by twist, it is convenient expand the conformal blocks as follows

\displaystyle f^{\Delta_{12}}_{\Delta,\ell}(z,{\bar{z}})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{q=-n}^{n}A_{n,q}(\Delta,\ell)\,(1-z)^{\frac{\Delta-\ell}{2}+n}\,\tilde{k}_{\frac{\Delta+\ell}{2}+q}^{\Delta_{12}}(1-{\bar{z}})\,.

(164)

Similar expansions have been used in the literature, for example
[
60
,
33
]
, but our chiral blocks take a somewhat unusual form:

\displaystyle\tilde{k}_{\bar{h}}^{\Delta_{12}}(1-{\bar{z}})=(1-{\bar{z}})^{\bar{h}}\,{\bar{z}}^{\Delta_{12}/4}\,_{2}F_{1}\left(\bar{h},\bar{h}+\frac{\Delta_{12}}{2};2\bar{h};1-{\bar{z}}\right)\,.

(165)

The coefficients
A_{n,q}(\Delta,\ell)
in the lightcone expansion could be fixed with (
A.2
), but in practice we found even more convenient to fix them directly from the Casimir equation
[
23
]

\displaystyle\Bigg[

\displaystyle(1-z)^{2}\left(z\partial_{z}^{2}+\partial_{z}-\frac{\Delta_{12}^{2}}{16z}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{z(1-z)(1-{\bar{z}})}{z-{\bar{z}}}\left(p\frac{(1-z)(1+{\bar{z}})}{1-z{\bar{z}}}-(d-2)\right)\partial_{z}+(z\leftrightarrow{\bar{z}})\Bigg]f_{\Delta,\ell}=\frac{c_{\Delta,\ell}}{2}f_{\Delta,\ell}\,,

(166)

where
c_{\Delta,\ell}=\Delta(\Delta-d)+\ell(\ell+d-2)
.
We are mostly interested in the leading-twist family of operators, for which only
A_{0,0}(\Delta,\ell)=1
is needed.
When we need the first subleading trajectory, the expansion follows from

\displaystyle A_{1,-1}(\Delta,\ell)

\displaystyle=\frac{\ell(d-2p-2)}{2\ell+d-4}\,,

(167)

\displaystyle A_{1,0}(\Delta,\ell)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta-\ell}{4}\,,

(168)

\displaystyle A_{1,1}(\Delta,\ell)

\displaystyle=\frac{(\Delta-1)(d-2p-2)(\Delta-\Delta_{12}+\ell)(\Delta+\Delta_{12}+\ell)}{16(2\Delta+2-d)(\Delta+\ell-1)(\Delta+\ell+1)}\,.

(169)

The coefficients
A_{n\geq 2,q}
can also be generated efficiently with the help of a computer.

C.1.2
Correlator
\langle\!\langle\phi\phi\rangle\!\rangle

First we should decompose the correlator
F_{\phi\phi}(r,w)
in the two symmetry channels (
51
), and then we should multiply by
\xi^{\Delta_{\phi}}
as in equation (
150
).
The simplest piece just gives a constant
\xi^{\Delta_{\phi}}\times\xi^{-{\Delta_{\phi}}}=1
, namely it is the conformal block of the bulk identity.
The term
H(r,w)
has been discussed in (
25
), and only corrects the scalar of dimension
\Delta=2
, namely
\phi^{2}
or
T_{ab}
.
Finally, the constant piece generates the expansion

\displaystyle\xi^{\Delta_{\phi}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\ell\text{ even}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\frac{\big(\frac{1}{2}{\Delta_{\phi}}\big)_{\ell/2}^{2}}{2^{\ell}\,\frac{\ell}{2}!\,\big(\frac{2{\Delta_{\phi}}+\ell-1}{2}\big)_{\ell/2}}\Bigg(f_{2{\Delta_{\phi}}+\ell,\ell}+\frac{(\ell+1)^{2}{\varepsilon}}{8(\ell+2)(2\ell+1)}f_{2{\Delta_{\phi}}+\ell+2,\ell}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\Bigg)\,.

(170)

In this expansion the blocks are evaluated in
d=4-{\varepsilon}
dimensions, and we have found that no higher-twist families besides
n=0,1
are involved.

C.1.3
Correlator
\langle\!\langle\phi\phi^{2}\rangle\!\rangle

For the
F_{\phi\phi^{2}}
correlator we should multiply by
\xi^{\frac{1}{2}({\Delta_{\phi}}+{\Delta_{\phi^{2}}})}
before expanding, and remember to use blocks with unequal external dimensions.
For example, the constant term in the correlator gives

\displaystyle\xi^{\frac{1}{2}({\Delta_{\phi}}+\Delta_{\phi^{2}})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\ell\text{ even}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\frac{\left(\frac{1}{2}{\Delta_{\phi}}\right)_{\ell/2}\left(\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\phi^{2}}\right)_{\ell/2}}{2^{\ell}\,\frac{\ell}{2}!\left(\frac{{\Delta_{\phi}}+\Delta_{\phi^{2}}+\ell-1}{2}\right)_{\ell/2}}\bigg(f^{\Delta_{\phi}-\Delta_{\phi^{2}}}_{\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\phi^{2}}+\ell,\ell}+\frac{{\varepsilon}}{16}f^{\Delta_{\phi}-\Delta_{\phi^{2}}}_{\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\phi^{2}}+\ell+2,\ell}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{(\ell+2)(\ell+6){\varepsilon}}{12288(\ell+3)(\ell+5)}f^{\Delta_{\phi}-\Delta_{\phi^{2}}}_{\Delta_{\phi}+\Delta_{\phi^{2}}+\ell+6,\ell}+\ldots\bigg)\,.

(171)

Note that
\ldots
stands for the higher-twist families that we omit, as well as corrections at
O({\varepsilon}^{2})
.

Next we consider the function
H(r,w)
. The expansion of
H(r,w)
follows from the combination of (
25
) with (
161
), but now it must match an expansion of blocks with unequal external dimensions.
Because
H(r,w)
is multiplied by
{\varepsilon}
, it is sufficient at the order we work to consider
d=4
and tree-level dimensions
{\Delta_{\phi}}-{\Delta_{\phi^{2}}}=-1
.
The result only involves the twist-three family

\displaystyle\xi^{3/2}\,H(r,w)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\ell\text{ even}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\frac{4^{-\ell}}{\ell+1}\left(\frac{1}{2}H_{\frac{\ell}{2}}-\frac{1}{2}H_{\frac{\ell+1}{2}}-\log 4+\partial_{\Delta}\right)f^{-1}_{3+\ell,\ell}\,.

(172)

To expand the remaining terms, remember that by multiplet recombination
\phi^{3}
becomes a descendant of
\phi
. As a result, the
{\varepsilon}
–expansion of
f_{{\Delta_{\phi}},0}^{{\Delta_{\phi}}-{\Delta_{\phi^{2}}}}
decomposes into blocks
f_{1,0}^{-1}
,
f_{3,0}^{-1}
and their derivatives.
This decomposition turns out to be very subtle.
In particular, we observed that it is sensitive to the values of scaling dimensions up to three-loops
[
85
]
:

\displaystyle{\Delta_{\phi}}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}+\frac{(N+2){\varepsilon}^{2}}{4(N+8)^{2}}\left(1-\frac{N^{2}-56N-272}{4(N+8)^{2}}\,{\varepsilon}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\right)\,,

(173)

\displaystyle{\Delta_{\phi^{2}}}

\displaystyle=2-{\varepsilon}+\frac{(N+2){\varepsilon}}{N+8}\left(1+\frac{13N+44}{2(N+8)^{2}}\,{\varepsilon}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\right)\,.

(174)

The lightcone expansion (
164
) of
f_{{\Delta_{\phi}},0}^{{\Delta_{\phi}}-{\Delta_{\phi^{2}}}}
can be compared with an ansatz of
f_{1,0}^{-1}
and
f_{3,0}^{-1}
blocks.
To simplify later calculations, it is useful to organize the result as

\displaystyle f_{{\Delta_{\phi}},0}^{{\Delta_{\phi}}-{\Delta_{\phi^{2}}}}(r,w)

\displaystyle=\xi^{\frac{1}{2}{\Delta_{\phi}}}-\frac{N+2}{2(N+8)}\,{\varepsilon}\,\xi^{1/2}\log\frac{4r}{(r+1)^{2}}-\frac{3+3\log 2}{4}\,{\varepsilon}\,f_{3,0}^{-1}+\frac{3{\varepsilon}}{4}\partial_{\Delta}f_{3,0}^{-1}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\frac{N+8}{8}+\frac{N^{2}-3N-22}{16(N+8)}\,{\varepsilon}+\frac{3\log 2}{4}\,{\varepsilon}\right)f_{{\Delta_{\phi}}+{\Delta_{\phi^{2}}},0}^{{\Delta_{\phi}}-{\Delta_{\phi^{2}}}}+O({\varepsilon}^{2})\,.

(175)

Note that the first two terms also correspond to blocks of the aforementioned form

\displaystyle\xi^{\frac{1}{2}{\Delta_{\phi}}}=f_{{\Delta_{\phi}},0}^{-{\Delta_{\phi}}}\,,\qquad\xi^{1/2}\,\log\frac{4r}{(1+r)^{2}}

\displaystyle=2\partial_{\Delta_{12}}f_{1,0}^{-1}-\frac{1}{8}f_{3,0}^{-1}\,,

(176)

but in practice it is easier to use them in the form (
175
).

C.1.4
Correlator
\langle\!\langle\phi^{2}\phi^{2}\rangle\!\rangle

This correlator should be multiplied by
\xi^{\Delta_{\phi^{2}}}
before expanding, so in particular the constant piece gives

\displaystyle\xi^{\Delta_{\phi^{2}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\ell\text{ even}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\frac{\left(\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\phi^{2}}\right)_{\frac{\ell}{2}}^{2}}{2^{\ell}\,\frac{\ell}{2}!\left(\frac{2\Delta_{\phi^{2}}+\ell-1}{2}\right)_{\frac{\ell}{2}}}\left(f_{2\Delta_{\phi^{2}}+\ell,\ell}+\frac{(\ell+2){\varepsilon}}{8(2\ell+3)}f_{2\Delta_{\phi^{2}}+\ell+2,\ell}+\ldots\right)\,,

(177)

where
\ldots
stand for higher-twist families as well as higher orders in
{\varepsilon}
.
Next there is a term
\xi^{{\Delta_{\phi}}-{\Delta_{\phi^{2}}}}
, but its expansion has already been presented in (
170
).
Out of the two terms involving
H(r,w)
, one is expanded using (
25
), while for the other we find

\displaystyle\xi^{2}\,H(r,w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\ell\text{ even}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\big({-}A_{\ell,n}+B_{\ell,n}\partial_{\Delta}\big)f_{4+\ell+2n,\ell}\,.

(178)

Again we are working at order
O({\varepsilon}^{0})
because this term is multiplied by
{\varepsilon}
in the correlator.
In this case, we have not found closed formulas for the coefficients
A_{\ell,n}
and
B_{\ell,n}
, although it is possible to generate them using certain recursion relations.
Since these coefficients do not play an important role in the main text, we content ourselves by presenting the lowest-lying data in tables
1
and
2
.
We observed empirically that for
n
odd the coefficients vanish.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c|l|l}\ell&A_{\ell,0}&A_{\ell,2}\\
\hline\cr 0&1+\log 2&\frac{11}{10368}+\frac{\log 2}{864}\\
2&\frac{101}{1800}+\frac{7\log 2}{120}&\frac{2473}{34992000}+\frac{31\log 2}{388800}\\
4&\frac{119521}{38102400}+\frac{407\log 2}{120960}&\frac{50109481}{11491749068800}+\frac{1437\log 2}{287006720}\\
6&\frac{124370867}{692583091200}+\frac{3023\log 2}{15375360}&\frac{7111918573}{26797814184960000}+\frac{59303\log 2}{192471552000}\\
8&\frac{1004193021703}{96075126411264000}+\frac{1456787\log 2}{125462937600}&\frac{7040698918075}{436903886309069684736}+\frac{9199735\log 2}{487966670585856}\\
10&\frac{143434115533099}{233070570663613562880}+\frac{11072177\log 2}{16019107872768}&\frac{14795257077824297}{15110820472963896705024000}+\frac{5202131\log 2}{4515554582855680}\end{array}

Table 1
:
Low-lying coefficients defined by equation (
178
).

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c|cccccc}\ell&0&2&4&6&8&10\\
\hline\cr B_{\ell,0}&1&\frac{7}{120}&\frac{407}{120960}&\frac{3023}{15375360}&\frac{1456787}{125462937600}&\frac{11072177}{16019107872768}\\
\hline\cr B_{\ell,2}&\frac{1}{864}&\frac{31}{388800}&\frac{1437}{287006720}&\frac{59303}{192471552000}&\frac{9199735}{487966670585856}&\frac{5202131}{4515554582855680}\end{array}

Table 2
:
Low-lying coefficients defined by equation (
178
).

Finally, the expansion of the logarithmic term at
{\varepsilon}=0
contains only twist-two and twist-four operators:

\displaystyle\xi\,\log\frac{4r}{(1+r)^{2}}=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\ell\text{ even}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\frac{(\ell!)^{2}}{8^{\ell}(\frac{\ell}{2}!)^{3}\left(\frac{\ell+1}{2}\right)_{\frac{\ell}{2}}}\left(H_{\ell}\,f_{2+\ell,\ell}+\frac{(\ell+1)^{2}}{4(\ell+2)(2\ell+1)}f_{4+\ell,\ell}\right)\,.

(179)

C.2
Defect channel

We now expand the same terms but in the defect channel with a similar strategy as above.
The only difference is that we consider the limit
|z|\ll|{\bar{z}}|\ll 1
, and the defect expansion is organized by transverse twist
{\widehat{\Delta}}-s
.
The defect CFT data is somewhat easier to extract than the bulk one, since we have closed form expressions for the blocks (
149
).
Note that we should no longer multiply the correlator by
\xi^{\frac{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}}{2}}
before expanding.

C.2.1
Correlator
\langle\!\langle\phi\phi\rangle\!\rangle

The constant term corresponds to the exchange of the defect identity
\widehat{f}_{0,0}=1
, while the expansion of
H(r,w)
appears in (
23
).
The only term left is the bulk identity, which is well-known in the literature
[
32
]

\displaystyle\xi^{-{\Delta_{\phi}}}=\sum_{n,s=0}^{\infty}\frac{\left({\Delta_{\phi}}-\frac{d-2}{2}\right)_{n}({\Delta_{\phi}})_{2n+s}}{s!n!\left(s+\frac{d-1}{2}\right)_{n}\left({\Delta_{\phi}}+s+n-\frac{1}{2}\right)_{n}}\widehat{f}_{{\Delta_{\phi}}+s+2n,s}\,.

(180)

C.2.2
Correlator
\langle\!\langle\phi\phi^{2}\rangle\!\rangle

The first three terms of
F_{\phi\phi^{2}}
can be treated identically as for
F_{\phi\phi}
, except that we should replace
{\Delta_{\phi}}\to\frac{1}{2}{\Delta_{\phi^{2}}}
in (
180
).
The expansion of the new logarithmic term is

\displaystyle\xi^{-1}\,\log\frac{4r}{(1+r)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\big(2\log 2+\partial_{\widehat{\Delta}}\big)\widehat{f}_{1+s,s}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\frac{4(-1)^{n}(s+1)_{n}}{n(2s+n+1)\left(\frac{2s+3}{2}\right)_{n-1}}\widehat{f}_{1+s+n,s}\,.

(181)

Again have set
{\varepsilon}=0
because the logarithmic term appears at order
O({\varepsilon})
in the correlator.

C.2.3
Correlator
\langle\!\langle\phi^{2}\phi^{2}\rangle\!\rangle

Finally, for the
F_{\phi^{2}\phi^{2}}
correlator the only missing ingredient is the expansion

\displaystyle\xi^{-1}\,H(r,w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\left(-\widehat{A}_{s,n}+\widehat{B}_{s,n}\partial_{\widehat{\Delta}}\right)\widehat{f}_{2+s+2n,s}\,.

(182)

The coefficients
\widehat{B}_{s,n}
are amenable to being guessed, and for the first two we find

\displaystyle\widehat{B}_{s,0}=H_{s+\frac{1}{2}}+\log 4\,,\quad\widehat{B}_{s,1}=-\frac{4(s+1)^{2}}{(2s+3)^{2}}+\frac{2\left(2s^{2}+8s+7\right)}{(2s+3)(2s+5)}\left(H_{s+\frac{1}{2}}+\log 4\right)\,.

(183)

On the other hand, we could not guess
\widehat{A}_{s,n}
, so instead we present some low-lying values in table
3
.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c|l|l}s&\widehat{A}_{s,0}&\widehat{A}_{s,1}\\
\hline\cr 0&4-4\log 2&\frac{1624}{675}-\frac{128\log 2}{45}\\
1&\frac{46}{9}-\frac{16\log 2}{3}&\frac{177328}{55125}-\frac{2048\log 2}{525}\\
2&\frac{1291}{225}-\frac{92\log 2}{15}&\frac{7733504}{2083725}-\frac{30208\log 2}{6615}\\
3&\frac{68044}{11025}-\frac{704\log 2}{105}&\frac{5711504}{1403325}-\frac{2048\log 2}{405}\\

\end{array}

:
Low-lying coefficients defined by equation (

).
